From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F43.1, F06.4\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well… I came in because I just feel like I can’t settle inside myself anymore. It’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic, but it’s like I’m in my own skin and it doesn’t feel quiet in there. Even when I’m tired — and I mean really worn out, where I know I should just sit down and rest — there’s this feeling like I have to get up, do the next thing, move on to something else, check something, start something.\n\nIt’s not just being “busy,” you know? It feels more like an inner push, like I’m being prodded from the inside. I’ll tell myself, “Just sit still for a minute,” and then almost right away I feel like I need to jump up again. It’s gotten to where I feel uncomfortable just being still, and that’s really what made me think I need to talk to someone, because I’m exhausted but I still can’t seem to settle.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had periods where you felt unusually energetic, needed less sleep, or felt like your thoughts were racing?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I wouldn’t say I’ve had, like, a clear stretch where I suddenly felt wonderful and full of energy and didn’t need sleep. That doesn’t sound quite like me.\n\nMy thoughts do feel busy sometimes, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean by “racing.” It’s more that I have a lot going on in my head and I have trouble slowing it down, but I can’t really point to a period where it was unusual in that way, with needing less sleep or feeling powered up. If anything, I’m often tired, even when I still can’t seem to settle.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe what happens when you try to relax or when you’re in a situation where you’re supposed to be still — do you feel restless, tense, or like something bad is going to happen?\n\nPatient: Yes… restless is probably the best word, though it almost doesn’t feel strong enough. It’s not so much that I think something bad is going to happen, exactly. It’s more like I just cannot get comfortable being still. Like I’m sitting there and my body is technically in the chair, but inside I’m already halfway up, if that makes sense.\n\nIf I try to relax, I’ll tell myself, “Just sit here, just rest for a bit, you’re tired,” because I really am worn out a lot of the time. But then almost immediately I get this urge to jump up and do the next thing — check something, straighten something, start some little task, anything. It feels like being in my own skin is uncomfortable when I’m not moving.\n\nAnd it’s frustrating because I know I should be able to sit still. I’m not trying to be busy for the sake of being busy. It feels more like there’s this pressure inside me pushing me along, even when I don’t have the energy for it. So yes, when I’m supposed to be still, I feel very restless — not peaceful, not settled. I feel like I have to get up.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel a lot of worry or fear that seems out of proportion to what's actually happening around you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think fear is really the word I’d use. I mean, I worry about things like anybody does, and sometimes I can get worked up in my head trying to sort things out, but I don’t usually feel like I’m scared of something that isn’t really there, if that’s what you mean.\n\nIt’s more… I don’t know, I’m trying to be accurate. I wouldn’t say I’m sitting there thinking something terrible is about to happen. It’s not like that kind of fear. If I seem wound up, it’s not usually because I believe there’s some big danger around me. It’s more that I feel unsettled, and then I might start fussing over little things because I can’t quite leave them alone.\n\nSo, no, I haven’t really noticed a lot of out-of-proportion fear. Worry, maybe sometimes, but not in a way where I’d say it’s the main thing or that it feels bigger than what’s happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods where you felt down, sad, or lost interest in things that usually matter to you?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean… I have days where I feel worn out or discouraged, especially when I can’t get myself together the way I think I should. But I don’t know if I’d call it being “down” in the way you mean.\n\nI still care about things, at least I think I do. It’s more that sometimes I don’t follow through or I put things off, and then from the outside maybe it could look like I’ve lost interest. But inside it doesn’t feel like I stopped caring. It’s more like I get stuck, or I can’t quite make myself start.\n\nSo… I don’t think I’ve had a clear period where everything felt sad and empty, or where things that matter to me suddenly didn’t matter. Not that I can point to, anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself moving around a lot, like pacing, fidgeting, or tapping your hands or feet, even when you're trying to stay still?\n\nPatient: Yes, absolutely. I’m doing it more than I realize half the time. Like even in church meetings, where you’re supposed to be quiet and respectful and still, I’ll catch myself tapping my foot, or rubbing the edge of my purse over and over with my fingers, or shifting around in the chair.\n\nAnd I try to stop because I don’t want to be distracting, especially sitting with the other ladies there, but then a few minutes later I’m doing something else with my hands or feet. Sometimes I notice because the ladies beside me start looking over, and then I feel embarrassed, like, “Oh, I’m doing it again.” It’s not that I’m trying to be rude or impatient. I just feel like my body keeps needing to move, even when I’m trying very hard to sit still.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed this inner restlessness or need to move getting worse when you start or change any medications?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can tie it neatly to starting or changing a medicine, no. I wish I could give you a clean answer like, “Yes, it happened right after this,” but that’s not really how I’ve been thinking of it.\n\nWhat I can say is that the reason I came in is because lately I feel like I just cannot settle inside my own skin. That’s the best way I know how to put it. It’s like there’s this push in me to get up and do the next thing, or fix the next thing, or move around, even when I’m worn out and I know I should just sit still. My body will be tired, but inside I’m still sort of… revved up, I guess.\n\nSo I don’t know if medication changes have made it worse or not. I haven’t noticed that pattern clearly. It’s more that the restlessness itself has gotten hard to ignore.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you had to move because of an uncomfortable feeling inside your body, even if you couldn’t quite explain what was bothering you?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that’s actually a good way to say it. It’s hard for me to explain because it’s not always like pain or something I can point to, like, “My leg hurts” or “my back hurts.” It’s more like this uncomfortable feeling inside me, like I cannot settle inside my own skin anymore.\n\nI’ll be sitting there, and I know I’m tired, I know I should just sit still for a minute, but there’s this feeling like I have to jump up and do the next thing. Like I need to move, or start something, or check on something, even if there isn’t really a good reason right then. It’s almost like my body and my mind don’t agree with each other. My body is worn out, but inside I feel pushed, like I can’t just be still.\n\nAnd it bothers me because I’ll think, “Why can’t I just sit here like a normal person?” I don’t even always know what I’m trying to fix or what I’m supposed to be doing. I just feel like I have to get up and do something. So yes, I think that’s been a big part of why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Is this inner restlessness something you feel most of the time, even when you're not in situations that might make you anxious or require you to sit still, like when you're alone at home relaxing?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s not only when I’m somewhere I’m supposed to sit still, like a meeting or church or something formal. It happens even when I’m home and there’s no one there to bother me and nothing I have to prove to anybody.\n\nThat’s actually part of what worries me, because I’ll be alone at home and I’ll think, “Okay, I can finally sit down and relax,” and then I just… can’t. I might be worn out, really tired, and still I feel like I need to get up and do the next thing. Not always because there’s some urgent thing that has to be done, but because I feel unsettled inside, like I can’t settle inside my own skin.\n\nIt’s like my body should be resting, but something in me is saying, “Get up, move, do something.” And I’ll sit for a minute and then I’m up again, or I’m thinking about what I should jump to next. I wish I could give you a clean answer like, “Yes, it happened right after this,” but that’s not really how I’ve been thinking of it.\n\nWhat I can say is that the reason I came in is because lately I feel like I just cannot settle inside my own skin. That’s the best way I know how to put it. It’s like there’s this push in me to get up and do the next thing, or fix the next thing, or move around, even when I’m worn out and I know I should just sit still. My body will be tired, but inside I’m still sort of… revved up, I guess.\n\nSo I don’t know if medication changes have made it worse or not. I haven’t noticed that pattern clearly. It’s more that the restlessness itself has gotten hard to ignore.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you had to move because of an uncomfortable feeling inside your body, even if you couldn’t quite explain what was bothering you?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that’s actually a good way to say it. It’s hard for me to explain because it’s not always like pain or something I can point to, like, “My leg hurts” or “my back hurts.” It’s more like this uncomfortable feeling inside me, like I cannot settle inside my own skin anymore.\n\nI’ll be sitting there, and I know I’m tired, I know I should just sit still for a minute, but there’s this feeling like I have to jump up and do the next thing. Like I need to move, or start something, or check on something, even if there isn’t really a good reason right then. It’s almost like my body and my mind don’t agree with each other. My body is worn out, but inside I feel pushed, like I can’t just be still.\n\nAnd it bothers me because I’ll think, “Why can’t I just sit here like a normal person?” I don’t even always know what I’m trying to fix or what I’m supposed to be doing. I just feel like I have to get up and do something. So yes, I think that’s been a big part of why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Is this inner restlessness something you feel most of the time, even when you're not in situations that might make you anxious or require you to sit still, like when you're alone at home relaxing?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s not only when I’m somewhere I’m supposed to sit still, like a meeting or church or something formal. It happens even when I’m home and there’s no one there to bother me and nothing I have to prove to anybody.\n\nThat’s actually part of what worries me, because I’ll be alone at home and I’ll think, “Okay, I can finally sit down and relax,” and then I just… can’t. I might be worn out, really tired, and still I feel like I need to get up and do the next thing. Not always because there’s some urgent thing that has to be done, but because I feel unsettled inside, like I can’t settle inside my own skin.\n\nIt’s like my body should be resting, but something in me is saying, “Get up, move, do something.” And I’ll sit for a minute and then I’m up again, or I’m thinking about what I should jump to next. So yes, it’s most of the time lately, not just in situations that make me nervous or where I’m being expected to sit still. That’s really why I came in, because it’s gotten to where I don’t feel like I can just be still anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this inner restlessness or need to move after taking any medications, especially ones like antidepressants or antipsychotics?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t know that I can say it happened after taking a certain medication. I don’t want to give you the wrong idea or act like I’ve clearly noticed, “Oh, I took this pill and then I couldn’t sit still,” because I haven’t really tracked it that way.\n\nWhat I can say is that the reason I came in is this feeling that I just cannot settle inside my own skin anymore. It’s like I have to jump up and do the next thing, even when I’m worn out and should be sitting still. I’ll be tired, and I’ll know there’s no reason I need to get up right that second, but inside it feels like something is pushing me to move or do something.\n\nSo, as far as antidepressants or those kinds of medicines, I can’t honestly connect it to that. The restlessness is definitely there, though, and it’s gotten hard for me to ignore.\n\nDoctor: Has this inner restlessness made it hard for you to function in your usual daily activities, or caused you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes, it really has. I wish I could say it was just annoying, like just this private uncomfortable feeling, but it’s gone past that.\n\nMy family has had to step in with things I used to handle myself, like bills and paperwork, because I either don’t sit down long enough to finish them or I jump from one thing to another and leave it half done. And then it piles up, and then I feel ashamed, and then someone has to help me sort through it. That’s been hard, because I’ve always wanted to be seen as someone who can manage her own affairs.\n\nAnd it’s affected other people too, not just at home. At church, and with the garden group, there have been times when I said I would take care of something or be responsible for part of a plan, and then I dropped it or didn’t follow through the way I should have. People had to redo plans or cover for me, and I could tell it frustrated them, even if they were kind about it.\n\nThat part really bothers me. I feel embarrassed, honestly. These are groups I care about, and I don’t want to be unreliable. But lately I feel like people don’t quite trust that they can count on me the way they used to, and that makes me feel awful. So yes, it’s caused a lot of distress, and it’s definitely interfering with my normal life.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced this kind of restlessness or need to move when you started, stopped, or changed the dose of a medication, even if it was a long time ago?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I’ve been trying to think back, because I know you’re asking whether there was some clear point where I started or stopped a medicine, or the dose changed, and then this restlessness showed up.\n\nBut I can’t really say that. I don’t have a memory of, “Oh, that happened right after a medication change.” I just don’t want to guess and accidentally make it sound like there’s a connection when I haven’t actually noticed one.\n\nI mean, it may be that there were medication changes over the years and I just didn’t pay attention closely enough, but as I’m sitting here trying to remember, nothing stands out to me like that. What stands out is the restlessness itself and how hard it’s been to settle down, but I can’t tie it to starting, stopping, or changing a dose of something.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel driven to do certain things over and over, like checking, counting, or fixing, because you can't get certain thoughts out of your head or because you feel something bad will happen if you don't?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in the way you’re describing. I mean, I do sometimes go back and recheck things, but I think that’s more because I get distracted or I don’t trust that I finished something properly, not because I have a specific thought like, “If I don’t check this three times, something terrible will happen.”\n\nI don’t really count things or have rituals like that, at least not that I’ve noticed. And I don’t feel like I’m fixing things over and over to stop some bad event from happening. It’s more that I’ll lose track, or I’ll feel unsettled and then I’m not sure what I already did. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, it goes way back. It’s not something that just started recently, even though it feels worse or more noticeable now.

I actually remember seeing comments from teachers when I was in grade school — or my mother would mention them — that I was “bright but careless,” that kind of thing. They’d say I lost papers, talked when I wasn’t supposed to, didn’t stay with my work, or needed reminders to stay in my seat and finish what I was doing. At the time, I think people just thought I was careless or not trying hard enough, because I could understand the schoolwork, but I was always being corrected for the way I went about it.

So yes, the sitting still and settling myself, that has been there since I was young. It’s just that when you’re a child, people scold you or redirect you, and you don’t necessarily think of it as a pattern until much later.

Even when I’m tired — and I mean really worn out, where I know I should just sit down and rest — there’s this feeling like I have to get up, do the next thing, move on to something else, check something, start something.\n\nIt’s not just being “busy,” you know? If anything, I’m often tired, even when I still can’t seem to settle.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe what happens when you try to relax or when you’re in a situation where you’re supposed to be still — do you feel restless, tense, or like something bad is going to happen?\n\nPatient: Yes… restless is probably the best word, though it almost doesn’t feel strong enough. It’s not so much that I think something bad is going to happen, exactly. It’s more like I just cannot get comfortable being still. Like I’m sitting there and my body is technically in the chair, but inside I’m already halfway up, if that makes sense.\n\nIf I try to relax, I’ll tell myself, “Just sit here, just rest for a bit, you’re tired,” because I really am worn out a lot of the time. But then almost immediately I get this urge to jump up and do the next thing — check something, straighten something, start some little task, anything. It feels like being in my own skin is uncomfortable when I’m not moving.\n\nAnd it’s frustrating because I know I should be able to sit still. I’m not trying to be busy for the sake of being busy. It feels more like there’s this pressure inside me pushing me along, even when I don’t have the energy for it. So yes, when I’m supposed to be still, I feel very restless — not peaceful, not settled. I feel like I have to get up.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel a lot of worry or fear that seems out of proportion to what's actually happening around you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think fear is really the word I’d use. I mean, I worry about things like anybody does, and sometimes I can get worked up in my head trying to sort things out, but I don’t usually feel like I’m scared of something that isn’t really there, if that’s what you mean.\n\nIt’s more… I don’t know, I’m trying to be accurate. I wouldn’t say I’m sitting there thinking something terrible is about to happen. It’s not like that kind of fear. If I seem wound up, it’s not usually because I believe there’s some big danger around me. It’s more that I feel unsettled, and then I might start fussing over little things because I can’t quite leave them alone.\n\nSo, no, I haven’t really noticed a lot of out-of-proportion fear. Worry, maybe sometimes, but not in a way where I’d say it’s the main thing or that it feels bigger than what’s happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods where you felt down, sad, or lost interest in things that usually matter to you?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean… I have days where I feel worn out or discouraged, especially when I can’t get myself together the way I think I should. But I don’t know if I’d call it being “down” in the way you mean.\n\nI still care about things, at least I think I do. It’s more that sometimes I don’t follow through or I put things off, and then from the outside maybe it could look like I’ve lost interest. But inside it doesn’t feel like I stopped caring. It’s more like I get stuck, or I can’t quite make myself start.\n\nSo… I don’t think I’ve had a clear period where everything felt sad and empty, or where things that matter to me suddenly didn’t matter. Not that I can point to, anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself moving around a lot, like pacing, fidgeting, or tapping your hands or feet, even when you're trying to stay still?\n\nPatient: Yes, absolutely. I’m doing it more than I realize half the time. Like even in church meetings, where you’re supposed to be quiet and respectful and still, I’ll catch myself tapping my foot, or rubbing the edge of my purse over and over with my fingers, or shifting around in the chair.\n\nAnd I try to stop because I don’t want to be distracting, especially sitting with the other ladies there, but then a few minutes later I’m doing something else with my hands or feet. Sometimes I notice because the ladies beside me start looking over, and then I feel embarrassed, like, “Oh, I’m doing it again.” It’s not that I’m trying to be rude or impatient. I just feel like my body keeps needing to move, even when I’m trying very hard to sit still.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed this inner restlessness or need to move getting worse when you start or change any medications?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can tie it neatly to starting or changing a medicine, no. I wish I could give you a clean answer like, “Yes, it happened right after this,” but that’s not really how I’ve been thinking of it.\n\nWhat I can say is that the reason I came in is because lately I feel like I just cannot settle inside my own skin. That’s the best way I know how to put it. It’s like there’s this push in me to get up and do the next thing, or fix the next thing, or move around, even when I’m worn out and I know I should just sit still. My body will be tired, but inside I’m still sort of… revved up, I guess.\n\nSo I don’t know if medication changes have made it worse or not. I haven’t noticed that pattern clearly. It’s more that the restlessness itself has gotten hard to ignore.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you had to move because of an uncomfortable feeling inside your body, even if you couldn’t quite explain what was bothering you?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that’s actually a good way to say it. It’s hard for me to explain because it’s not always like pain or something I can point to, like, “My leg hurts” or “my back hurts.” It’s more like this uncomfortable feeling inside me, like I cannot settle inside my own skin anymore.\n\nI’ll be sitting there, and I know I’m tired, I know I should just sit still for a minute, but there’s this feeling like I have to jump up and do the next thing. Like I need to move, or start something, or check on something, even if there isn’t really a good reason right then. It’s almost like my body and my mind don’t agree with each other. My body is worn out, but inside I feel pushed, like I can’t just be still.\n\nAnd it bothers me because I’ll think, “Why can’t I just sit here like a normal person?” I don’t even always know what I’m trying to fix or what I’m supposed to be doing. I just feel like I have to get up and do something. So yes, I think that’s been a big part of why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Is this inner restlessness something you feel most of the time, even when you're not in situations that might make you anxious or require you to sit still, like when you're alone at home relaxing?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s not only when I’m somewhere I’m supposed to sit still, like a meeting or church or something formal. It happens even when I’m home and there’s no one there to bother me and nothing I have to prove to anybody.\n\nThat’s actually part of what worries me, because I’ll be alone at home and I’ll think, “Okay, I can finally sit down and relax,” and then I just… can’t. I might be worn out, really tired, and still I feel like I need to get up and do the next thing. Not always because there’s some urgent thing that has to be done, but because I feel unsettled inside, like I can’t settle inside my own skin.\n\nIt’s like my body should be resting, but something in me is saying, “Get up, move, do something.” And I’ll sit for a minute and then I’m up again, or I’m thinking about what I should jump to next. So yes, it’s most of the time lately, not just in situations that make me nervous or where I’m being expected to sit still. That’s really why I came in, because it’s gotten to where I don’t feel like I can just be still anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this inner restlessness or need to move after taking any medications, especially ones like antidepressants or antipsychotics?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t know that I can say it happened after taking a certain medication. I don’t want to give you the wrong idea or act like I’ve clearly noticed, “Oh, I took this pill and then I couldn’t sit still,” because I haven’t really tracked it that way.\n\nWhat I can say is that the reason I came in is this feeling that I just cannot settle inside my own skin anymore. It’s like I have to jump up and do the next thing, even when I’m worn out and should be sitting still. I’ll be tired, and I’ll know there’s no reason I need to get up right that second, but inside it feels like something is pushing me to move or do something.\n\nSo, as far as antidepressants or those kinds of medicines, I can’t honestly connect it to that. The restlessness is definitely there, though, and it’s gotten hard for me to ignore.\n\nDoctor: Has this inner restlessness made it hard for you to function in your usual daily activities, or caused you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes, it really has. I wish I could say it was just annoying, like just this private uncomfortable feeling, but it’s gone past that.\n\nMy family has had to step in with things I used to handle myself, like bills and paperwork, because I either don’t sit down long enough to finish them or I jump from one thing to another and leave it half done. And then it piles up, and then I feel ashamed, and then someone has to help me sort through it. That’s been hard, because I’ve always wanted to be seen as someone who can manage her own affairs.\n\nAnd it’s affected other people too, not just at home. At church, and with the garden group, there have been times when I said I would take care of something or be responsible for part of a plan, and then I dropped it or didn’t follow through the way I should have. People had to redo plans or cover for me, and I could tell it frustrated them, even if they were kind about it.\n\nThat part really bothers me. I feel embarrassed, honestly. These are groups I care about, and I don’t want to be unreliable. But lately I feel like people don’t quite trust that they can count on me the way they used to, and that makes me feel awful. So yes, it’s caused a lot of distress, and it’s definitely interfering with my normal life.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced this kind of restlessness or need to move when you started, stopped, or changed the dose of a medication, even if it was a long time ago?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I’ve been trying to think back, because I know you’re asking whether there was some clear point where I started or stopped a medicine, or the dose changed, and then this restlessness showed up.\n\nBut I can’t really say that. I don’t have a memory of, “Oh, that happened right after a medication change.” I just don’t want to guess and accidentally make it sound like there’s a connection when I haven’t actually noticed one.\n\nI mean, it may be that there were medication changes over the years and I just didn’t pay attention closely enough, but as I’m sitting here trying to remember, nothing stands out to me like that. What stands out is the restlessness itself and how hard it’s been to settle down, but I can’t tie it to starting, stopping, or changing a dose of something.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel driven to do certain things over and over, like checking, counting, or fixing, because you can't get certain thoughts out of your head or because you feel something bad will happen if you don't?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in the way you’re describing. I mean, I do sometimes go back and recheck things, but I think that’s more because I get distracted or I don’t trust that I finished something properly, not because I have a specific thought like, “If I don’t check this three times, something terrible will happen.”\n\nI don’t really count things or have rituals like that, at least not that I’ve noticed. And I don’t feel like I’m fixing things over and over to stop some bad event from happening. It’s more that I’ll lose track, or I’ll feel unsettled and then I’m not sure what I already did. But I can’t say I have those stuck thoughts you’re talking about, where they force me to do a certain action in a certain way.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble sitting still or feeling calm, even going back to when you were younger, or is this something that started more recently in your life?\n\nPatient: Oh, it goes way back. It’s not something that just started recently, even though it feels worse or more noticeable now.\n\nI actually remember seeing comments from teachers when I was in grade school — or my mother would mention them — that I was “bright but careless,” that kind of thing. They’d say I lost papers, talked when I wasn’t supposed to, didn’t stay with my work, or needed reminders to stay in my seat and finish what I was doing. At the time, I think people just thought I was careless or not trying hard enough, because I could understand the schoolwork, but I was always being corrected for the way I went about it.\n\nSo yes, the sitting still and settling myself, that has been there since I was young. It’s just that when you’re a child, people scold you or redirect you, and you don’t necessarily think of it as a pattern until much later.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so, no. I mean, I’ve had times where I felt keyed up or couldn’t settle, but not in the way you’re saying — like several days where I was unusually upbeat or full of confidence and barely sleeping and still feeling great.

I’m trying to be careful, because my energy can feel all over the place, and sometimes I’ll stay up too late or get wound up, but I don’t remember a stretch where I thought, “Wow, I don’t need sleep,” or where I was on top of the world for days. Even when I’m tired — and I mean really worn out, where I know I should just sit down and rest — there’s this feeling like I have to get up, do the next thing, move on to something else, check something, start something.\n\nIt’s not just being “busy,” you know? It feels more like an inner push, like I’m being prodded from the inside. I’ll tell myself, “Just sit still for a minute,” and then almost right away I feel like I need to jump up again. It’s gotten to where I feel uncomfortable just being still, and that’s really what made me think I need to talk to someone, because I’m exhausted but I still can’t seem to settle.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had periods where you felt unusually energetic, needed less sleep, or felt like your thoughts were racing?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I wouldn’t say I’ve had, like, a clear stretch where I suddenly felt wonderful and full of energy and didn’t need sleep. That doesn’t sound quite like me.\n\nMy thoughts do feel busy sometimes, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean by “racing.” It’s more that I have a lot going on in my head and I have trouble slowing it down, but I can’t really point to a period where it was unusual in that way, with needing less sleep or feeling powered up. If anything, I’m often tired, even when I still can’t seem to settle.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe what happens when you try to relax or when you’re in a situation where you’re supposed to be still — do you feel restless, tense, or like something bad is going to happen?\n\nPatient: Yes… restless is probably the best word, though it almost doesn’t feel strong enough. It’s not so much that I think something bad is going to happen, exactly. It’s more like I just cannot get comfortable being still. Like I’m sitting there and my body is technically in the chair, but inside I’m already halfway up, if that makes sense.\n\nIf I try to relax, I’ll tell myself, “Just sit here, just rest for a bit, you’re tired,” because I really am worn out a lot of the time. But then almost immediately I get this urge to jump up and do the next thing — check something, straighten something, start some little task, anything. It feels like being in my own skin is uncomfortable when I’m not moving.\n\nAnd it’s frustrating because I know I should be able to sit still. I’m not trying to be busy for the sake of being busy. It feels more like there’s this pressure inside me pushing me along, even when I don’t have the energy for it. So yes, when I’m supposed to be still, I feel very restless — not peaceful, not settled. I feel like I have to get up.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel a lot of worry or fear that seems out of proportion to what's actually happening around you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think fear is really the word I’d use. I mean, I worry about things like anybody does, and sometimes I can get worked up in my head trying to sort things out, but I don’t usually feel like I’m scared of something that isn’t really there, if that’s what you mean.\n\nIt’s more… I don’t know, I’m trying to be accurate. I wouldn’t say I’m sitting there thinking something terrible is about to happen. It’s not like that kind of fear. If I seem wound up, it’s not usually because I believe there’s some big danger around me. It’s more that I feel unsettled, and then I might start fussing over little things because I can’t quite leave them alone.\n\nSo, no, I haven’t really noticed a lot of out-of-proportion fear. Worry, maybe sometimes, but not in a way where I’d say it’s the main thing or that it feels bigger than what’s happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods where you felt down, sad, or lost interest in things that usually matter to you?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean… I have days where I feel worn out or discouraged, especially when I can’t get myself together the way I think I should. But I don’t know if I’d call it being “down” in the way you mean.\n\nI still care about things, at least I think I do. It’s more that sometimes I don’t follow through or I put things off, and then from the outside maybe it could look like I’ve lost interest. But inside it doesn’t feel like I stopped caring. It’s more like I get stuck, or I can’t quite make myself start.\n\nSo… I don’t think I’ve had a clear period where everything felt sad and empty, or where things that matter to me suddenly didn’t matter. Not that I can point to, anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself moving around a lot, like pacing, fidgeting, or tapping your hands or feet, even when you're trying to stay still?\n\nPatient: Yes, absolutely. I’m doing it more than I realize half the time. Like even in church meetings, where you’re supposed to be quiet and respectful and still, I’ll catch myself tapping my foot, or rubbing the edge of my purse over and over with my fingers, or shifting around in the chair.\n\nAnd I try to stop because I don’t want to be distracting, especially sitting with the other ladies there, but then a few minutes later I’m doing something else with my hands or feet. Sometimes I notice because the ladies beside me start looking over, and then I feel embarrassed, like, “Oh, I’m doing it again.” It’s not that I’m trying to be rude or impatient. I just feel like my body keeps needing to move, even when I’m trying very hard to sit still.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed this inner restlessness or need to move getting worse when you start or change any medications?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can tie it neatly to starting or changing a medicine, no. I wish I could give you a clean answer like, “Yes, it happened right after this,” but that’s not really how I’ve been thinking of it.\n\nWhat I can say is that the reason I came in is because lately I feel like I just cannot settle inside my own skin. That’s the best way I know how to put it. It’s like there’s this push in me to get up and do the next thing, or fix the next thing, or move around, even when I’m worn out and I know I should just sit still. My body will be tired, but inside I’m still sort of… revved up, I guess.\n\nSo I don’t know if medication changes have made it worse or not. I haven’t noticed that pattern clearly. It’s more that the restlessness itself has gotten hard to ignore.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you had to move because of an uncomfortable feeling inside your body, even if you couldn’t quite explain what was bothering you?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that’s actually a good way to say it. It’s hard for me to explain because it’s not always like pain or something I can point to, like, “My leg hurts” or “my back hurts.” It’s more like this uncomfortable feeling inside me, like I cannot settle inside my own skin anymore.\n\nI’ll be sitting there, and I know I’m tired, I know I should just sit still for a minute, but there’s this feeling like I have to jump up and do the next thing. Like I need to move, or start something, or check on something, even if there isn’t really a good reason right then. It’s almost like my body and my mind don’t agree with each other. My body is worn out, but inside I feel pushed, like I can’t just be still.\n\nAnd it bothers me because I’ll think, “Why can’t I just sit here like a normal person?” I don’t even always know what I’m trying to fix or what I’m supposed to be doing. I just feel like I have to get up and do something. So yes, I think that’s been a big part of why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Is this inner restlessness something you feel most of the time, even when you're not in situations that might make you anxious or require you to sit still, like when you're alone at home relaxing?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s not only when I’m somewhere I’m supposed to sit still, like a meeting or church or something formal. It happens even when I’m home and there’s no one there to bother me and nothing I have to prove to anybody.\n\nThat’s actually part of what worries me, because I’ll be alone at home and I’ll think, “Okay, I can finally sit down and relax,” and then I just… can’t. I might be worn out, really tired, and still I feel like I need to get up and do the next thing. Not always because there’s some urgent thing that has to be done, but because I feel unsettled inside, like I can’t settle inside my own skin.\n\nIt’s like my body should be resting, but something in me is saying, “Get up, move, do something.” And I’ll sit for a minute and then I’m up again, or I’m thinking about what I should jump to next. So yes, it’s most of the time lately, not just in situations that make me nervous or where I’m being expected to sit still. That’s really why I came in, because it’s gotten to where I don’t feel like I can just be still anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this inner restlessness or need to move after taking any medications, especially ones like antidepressants or antipsychotics?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t know that I can say it happened after taking a certain medication. I don’t want to give you the wrong idea or act like I’ve clearly noticed, “Oh, I took this pill and then I couldn’t sit still,” because I haven’t really tracked it that way.\n\nWhat I can say is that the reason I came in is this feeling that I just cannot settle inside my own skin anymore. It’s like I have to jump up and do the next thing, even when I’m worn out and should be sitting still. I’ll be tired, and I’ll know there’s no reason I need to get up right that second, but inside it feels like something is pushing me to move or do something.\n\nSo, as far as antidepressants or those kinds of medicines, I can’t honestly connect it to that. The restlessness is definitely there, though, and it’s gotten hard for me to ignore.\n\nDoctor: Has this inner restlessness made it hard for you to function in your usual daily activities, or caused you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes, it really has. I wish I could say it was just annoying, like just this private uncomfortable feeling, but it’s gone past that.\n\nMy family has had to step in with things I used to handle myself, like bills and paperwork, because I either don’t sit down long enough to finish them or I jump from one thing to another and leave it half done. And then it piles up, and then I feel ashamed, and then someone has to help me sort through it. That’s been hard, because I’ve always wanted to be seen as someone who can manage her own affairs.\n\nAnd it’s affected other people too, not just at home. At church, and with the garden group, there have been times when I said I would take care of something or be responsible for part of a plan, and then I dropped it or didn’t follow through the way I should have. People had to redo plans or cover for me, and I could tell it frustrated them, even if they were kind about it.\n\nThat part really bothers me. I feel embarrassed, honestly. These are groups I care about, and I don’t want to be unreliable. But lately I feel like people don’t quite trust that they can count on me the way they used to, and that makes me feel awful. So yes, it’s caused a lot of distress, and it’s definitely interfering with my normal life.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced this kind of restlessness or need to move when you started, stopped, or changed the dose of a medication, even if it was a long time ago?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I’ve been trying to think back, because I know you’re asking whether there was some clear point where I started or stopped a medicine, or the dose changed, and then this restlessness showed up.\n\nBut I can’t really say that. I don’t have a memory of, “Oh, that happened right after a medication change.” I just don’t want to guess and accidentally make it sound like there’s a connection when I haven’t actually noticed one.\n\nI mean, it may be that there were medication changes over the years and I just didn’t pay attention closely enough, but as I’m sitting here trying to remember, nothing stands out to me like that. What stands out is the restlessness itself and how hard it’s been to settle down, but I can’t tie it to starting, stopping, or changing a dose of something.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel driven to do certain things over and over, like checking, counting, or fixing, because you can't get certain thoughts out of your head or because you feel something bad will happen if you don't?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in the way you’re describing. I mean, I do sometimes go back and recheck things, but I think that’s more because I get distracted or I don’t trust that I finished something properly, not because I have a specific thought like, “If I don’t check this three times, something terrible will happen.”\n\nI don’t really count things or have rituals like that, at least not that I’ve noticed. And I don’t feel like I’m fixing things over and over to stop some bad event from happening. It’s more that I’ll lose track, or I’ll feel unsettled and then I’m not sure what I already did. But I can’t say I have those stuck thoughts you’re talking about, where they force me to do a certain action in a certain way.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble sitting still or feeling calm, even going back to when you were younger, or is this something that started more recently in your life?\n\nPatient: Oh, it goes way back. It’s not something that just started recently, even though it feels worse or more noticeable now.\n\nI actually remember seeing comments from teachers when I was in grade school — or my mother would mention them — that I was “bright but careless,” that kind of thing. They’d say I lost papers, talked when I wasn’t supposed to, didn’t stay with my work, or needed reminders to stay in my seat and finish what I was doing. At the time, I think people just thought I was careless or not trying hard enough, because I could understand the schoolwork, but I was always being corrected for the way I went about it.\n\nSo yes, the sitting still and settling myself, that has been there since I was young. It’s just that when you’re a child, people scold you or redirect you, and you don’t necessarily think of it as a pattern until much later.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period lasting several days or more where you felt extremely upbeat, unusually confident, or so energetic that you hardly needed sleep?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean, I’ve had times where I felt keyed up or couldn’t settle, but not in the way you’re saying — like several days where I was unusually upbeat or full of confidence and barely sleeping and still feeling great.\n\nI’m trying to be careful, because my energy can feel all over the place, and sometimes I’ll stay up too late or get wound up, but I don’t remember a stretch where I thought, “Wow, I don’t need sleep,” or where I was on top of the world for days. If anything, when I don’t sleep enough, I usually feel worse, not better.\n\nSo as far as I can tell, no, I haven’t had that kind of period.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I don’t think I’ve had a time like that. I mean, I can get irritable, and I can talk more when I’m wound up or nervous or trying to explain myself, but I don’t remember it coming in a several-day stretch where my mood was unusually high or I felt like a different person.

And I don’t think I’ve had those times where I was suddenly doing everything faster, taking risks, or feeling unusually good in a way that other people would notice as odd. If I’m moving quickly or talking a lot, it’s usually more scattered than anything, not like I’m confident and energized for days. I’m trying to think back, but nothing like that really stands out to me.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F43.1, F06.4\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well… I came in because I just feel like I can’t settle inside myself anymore. It’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic, but it’s like I’m in my own skin and it doesn’t feel quiet in there. Even when I’m tired — and I mean really worn out, where I know I should just sit down and rest — there’s this feeling like I have to get up, do the next thing, move on to something else, check something, start something.\n\nIt’s not just being “busy,” you know? It feels more like an inner push, like I’m being prodded from the inside. I’ll tell myself, “Just sit still for a minute,” and then almost right away I feel like I need to jump up again. It’s gotten to where I feel uncomfortable just being still, and that’s really what made me think I need to talk to someone, because I’m exhausted but I still can’t seem to settle.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had periods where you felt unusually energetic, needed less sleep, or felt like your thoughts were racing?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I wouldn’t say I’ve had, like, a clear stretch where I suddenly felt wonderful and full of energy and didn’t need sleep. That doesn’t sound quite like me.\n\nMy thoughts do feel busy sometimes, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean by “racing.” It’s more that I have a lot going on in my head and I have trouble slowing it down, but I can’t really point to a period where it was unusual in that way, with needing less sleep or feeling powered up. If anything, I’m often tired, even when I still can’t seem to settle.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe what happens when you try to relax or when you’re in a situation where you’re supposed to be still — do you feel restless, tense, or like something bad is going to happen?\n\nPatient: Yes… restless is probably the best word, though it almost doesn’t feel strong enough. It’s not so much that I think something bad is going to happen, exactly. It’s more like I just cannot get comfortable being still. Like I’m sitting there and my body is technically in the chair, but inside I’m already halfway up, if that makes sense.\n\nIf I try to relax, I’ll tell myself, “Just sit here, just rest for a bit, you’re tired,” because I really am worn out a lot of the time. But then almost immediately I get this urge to jump up and do the next thing — check something, straighten something, start some little task, anything. It feels like being in my own skin is uncomfortable when I’m not moving.\n\nAnd it’s frustrating because I know I should be able to sit still. I’m not trying to be busy for the sake of being busy. It feels more like there’s this pressure inside me pushing me along, even when I don’t have the energy for it. So yes, when I’m supposed to be still, I feel very restless — not peaceful, not settled. I feel like I have to get up.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel a lot of worry or fear that seems out of proportion to what's actually happening around you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think fear is really the word I’d use. I mean, I worry about things like anybody does, and sometimes I can get worked up in my head trying to sort things out, but I don’t usually feel like I’m scared of something that isn’t really there, if that’s what you mean.\n\nIt’s more… I don’t know, I’m trying to be accurate. I wouldn’t say I’m sitting there thinking something terrible is about to happen. It’s not like that kind of fear. If I seem wound up, it’s not usually because I believe there’s some big danger around me. It’s more that I feel unsettled, and then I might start fussing over little things because I can’t quite leave them alone.\n\nSo, no, I haven’t really noticed a lot of out-of-proportion fear. Worry, maybe sometimes, but not in a way where I’d say it’s the main thing or that it feels bigger than what’s happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods where you felt down, sad, or lost interest in things that usually matter to you?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean… I have days where I feel worn out or discouraged, especially when I can’t get myself together the way I think I should. But I don’t know if I’d call it being “down” in the way you mean.\n\nI still care about things, at least I think I do. It’s more that sometimes I don’t follow through or I put things off, and then from the outside maybe it could look like I’ve lost interest. But inside it doesn’t feel like I stopped caring. It’s more like I get stuck, or I can’t quite make myself start.\n\nSo… I don’t think I’ve had a clear period where everything felt sad and empty, or where things that matter to me suddenly didn’t matter. Not that I can point to, anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself moving around a lot, like pacing, fidgeting, or tapping your hands or feet, even when you're trying to stay still?\n\nPatient: Yes, absolutely. I’m doing it more than I realize half the time. Like even in church meetings, where you’re supposed to be quiet and respectful and still, I’ll catch myself tapping my foot, or rubbing the edge of my purse over and over with my fingers, or shifting around in the chair.\n\nAnd I try to stop because I don’t want to be distracting, especially sitting with the other ladies there, but then a few minutes later I’m doing something else with my hands or feet. Sometimes I notice because the ladies beside me start looking over, and then I feel embarrassed, like, “Oh, I’m doing it again.” It’s not that I’m trying to be rude or impatient. I just feel like my body keeps needing to move, even when I’m trying very hard to sit still.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed this inner restlessness or need to move getting worse when you start or change any medications?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can tie it neatly to starting or changing a medicine, no. I wish I could give you a clean answer like, “Yes, it happened right after this,” but that’s not really how I’ve been thinking of it.\n\nWhat I can say is that the reason I came in is because lately I feel like I just cannot settle inside my own skin. That’s the best way I know how to put it. It’s like there’s this push in me to get up and do the next thing, or fix the next thing, or move around, even when I’m worn out and I know I should just sit still. My body will be tired, but inside I’m still sort of… revved up, I guess.\n\nSo I don’t know if medication changes have made it worse or not. I haven’t noticed that pattern clearly. It’s more that the restlessness itself has gotten hard to ignore.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you had to move because of an uncomfortable feeling inside your body, even if you couldn’t quite explain what was bothering you?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that’s actually a good way to say it. It’s hard for me to explain because it’s not always like pain or something I can point to, like, “My leg hurts” or “my back hurts.” It’s more like this uncomfortable feeling inside me, like I cannot settle inside my own skin anymore.\n\nI’ll be sitting there, and I know I’m tired, I know I should just sit still for a minute, but there’s this feeling like I have to jump up and do the next thing. Like I need to move, or start something, or check on something, even if there isn’t really a good reason right then. It’s almost like my body and my mind don’t agree with each other. My body is worn out, but inside I feel pushed, like I can’t just be still.\n\nAnd it bothers me because I’ll think, “Why can’t I just sit here like a normal person?” I don’t even always know what I’m trying to fix or what I’m supposed to be doing. I just feel like I have to get up and do something. So yes, I think that’s been a big part of why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Is this inner restlessness something you feel most of the time, even when you're not in situations that might make you anxious or require you to sit still, like when you're alone at home relaxing?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s not only when I’m somewhere I’m supposed to sit still, like a meeting or church or something formal. It happens even when I’m home and there’s no one there to bother me and nothing I have to prove to anybody.\n\nThat’s actually part of what worries me, because I’ll be alone at home and I’ll think, “Okay, I can finally sit down and relax,” and then I just… can’t. I might be worn out, really tired, and still I feel like I need to get up and do the next thing. Not always because there’s some urgent thing that has to be done, but because I feel unsettled inside, like I can’t settle inside my own skin.\n\nIt’s like my body should be resting, but something in me is saying, “Get up, move, do something.” And I’ll sit for a minute and then I’m up again, or I’m thinking about what I should jump to next. So yes, it’s most of the time lately, not just in situations that make me nervous or where I’m being expected to sit still. That’s really why I came in, because it’s gotten to where I don’t feel like I can just be still anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this inner restlessness or need to move after taking any medications, especially ones like antidepressants or antipsychotics?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t know that I can say it happened after taking a certain medication. I don’t want to give you the wrong idea or act like I’ve clearly noticed, “Oh, I took this pill and then I couldn’t sit still,” because I haven’t really tracked it that way.\n\nWhat I can say is that the reason I came in is this feeling that I just cannot settle inside my own skin anymore. It’s like I have to jump up and do the next thing, even when I’m worn out and should be sitting still. I’ll be tired, and I’ll know there’s no reason I need to get up right that second, but inside it feels like something is pushing me to move or do something.\n\nSo, as far as antidepressants or those kinds of medicines, I can’t honestly connect it to that. The restlessness is definitely there, though, and it’s gotten hard for me to ignore.\n\nDoctor: Has this inner restlessness made it hard for you to function in your usual daily activities, or caused you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes, it really has. I wish I could say it was just annoying, like just this private uncomfortable feeling, but it’s gone past that.\n\nMy family has had to step in with things I used to handle myself, like bills and paperwork, because I either don’t sit down long enough to finish them or I jump from one thing to another and leave it half done. And then it piles up, and then I feel ashamed, and then someone has to help me sort through it. That’s been hard, because I’ve always wanted to be seen as someone who can manage her own affairs.\n\nAnd it’s affected other people too, not just at home. At church, and with the garden group, there have been times when I said I would take care of something or be responsible for part of a plan, and then I dropped it or didn’t follow through the way I should have. People had to redo plans or cover for me, and I could tell it frustrated them, even if they were kind about it.\n\nThat part really bothers me. I feel embarrassed, honestly. These are groups I care about, and I don’t want to be unreliable. But lately I feel like people don’t quite trust that they can count on me the way they used to, and that makes me feel awful. So yes, it’s caused a lot of distress, and it’s definitely interfering with my normal life.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced this kind of restlessness or need to move when you started, stopped, or changed the dose of a medication, even if it was a long time ago?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I’ve been trying to think back, because I know you’re asking whether there was some clear point where I started or stopped a medicine, or the dose changed, and then this restlessness showed up.\n\nBut I can’t really say that. I don’t have a memory of, “Oh, that happened right after a medication change.” I just don’t want to guess and accidentally make it sound like there’s a connection when I haven’t actually noticed one.\n\nI mean, it may be that there were medication changes over the years and I just didn’t pay attention closely enough, but as I’m sitting here trying to remember, nothing stands out to me like that. What stands out is the restlessness itself and how hard it’s been to settle down, but I can’t tie it to starting, stopping, or changing a dose of something.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel driven to do certain things over and over, like checking, counting, or fixing, because you can't get certain thoughts out of your head or because you feel something bad will happen if you don't?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in the way you’re describing. I mean, I do sometimes go back and recheck things, but I think that’s more because I get distracted or I don’t trust that I finished something properly, not because I have a specific thought like, “If I don’t check this three times, something terrible will happen.”\n\nI don’t really count things or have rituals like that, at least not that I’ve noticed. And I don’t feel like I’m fixing things over and over to stop some bad event from happening. It’s more that I’ll lose track, or I’ll feel unsettled and then I’m not sure what I already did. But I can’t say I have those stuck thoughts you’re talking about, where they force me to do a certain action in a certain way.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble sitting still or feeling calm, even going back to when you were younger, or is this something that started more recently in your life?\n\nPatient: Oh, it goes way back. It’s not something that just started recently, even though it feels worse or more noticeable now.\n\nI actually remember seeing comments from teachers when I was in grade school — or my mother would mention them — that I was “bright but careless,” that kind of thing. They’d say I lost papers, talked when I wasn’t supposed to, didn’t stay with my work, or needed reminders to stay in my seat and finish what I was doing. At the time, I think people just thought I was careless or not trying hard enough, because I could understand the schoolwork, but I was always being corrected for the way I went about it.\n\nSo yes, the sitting still and settling myself, that has been there since I was young. It’s just that when you’re a child, people scold you or redirect you, and you don’t necessarily think of it as a pattern until much later.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period lasting several days or more where you felt extremely upbeat, unusually confident, or so energetic that you hardly needed sleep?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean, I’ve had times where I felt keyed up or couldn’t settle, but not in the way you’re saying — like several days where I was unusually upbeat or full of confidence and barely sleeping and still feeling great.\n\nI’m trying to be careful, because my energy can feel all over the place, and sometimes I’ll stay up too late or get wound up, but I don’t remember a stretch where I thought, “Wow, I don’t need sleep,” or where I was on top of the world for days. If anything, when I don’t sleep enough, I usually feel worse, not better.\n\nSo as far as I can tell, no, I haven’t had that kind of period.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when your mood was elevated, you felt unusually good or irritable for several days, and you were doing things more quickly, taking more chances, or talking more than usual?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve had a time like that. I mean, I can get irritable, and I can talk more when I’m wound up or nervous or trying to explain myself, but I don’t remember it coming in a several-day stretch where my mood was unusually high or I felt like a different person.\n\nAnd I don’t think I’ve had those times where I was suddenly doing everything faster, taking risks, or feeling unusually good in a way that other people would notice as odd. If I’m moving quickly or talking a lot, it’s usually more scattered than anything, not like I’m confident and energized for days. I’m trying to think back, but nothing like that really stands out to me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I don’t want to give you the wrong idea or act like I’ve clearly noticed, “Oh, I took this pill and then I couldn’t sit still,” because I haven’t really tracked it that way.\n\nWhat I can say is that the reason I came in is this feeling that I just cannot settle inside my own skin anymore. It’s like I have to jump up and do the next thing, even when I’m worn out and should be sitting still. I’ll be tired, and I’ll know there’s no reason I need to get up right that second, but inside it feels like something is pushing me to move or do something.\n\nSo, as far as antidepressants or those kinds of medicines, I can’t honestly connect it to that. The restlessness is definitely there, though, and it’s gotten hard for me to ignore.\n\nDoctor: Has this inner restlessness made it hard for you to function in your usual daily activities, or caused you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes, it really has. I wish I could say it was just annoying, like just this private uncomfortable feeling, but it’s gone past that.\n\nMy family has had to step in with things I used to handle myself, like bills and paperwork, because I either don’t sit down long enough to finish them or I jump from one thing to another and leave it half done. And then it piles up, and then I feel ashamed, and then someone has to help me sort through it. That’s been hard, because I’ve always wanted to be seen as someone who can manage her own affairs.\n\nAnd it’s affected other people too, not just at home. At church, and with the garden group, there have been times when I said I would take care of something or be responsible for part of a plan, and then I dropped it or didn’t follow through the way I should have. People had to redo plans or cover for me, and I could tell it frustrated them, even if they were kind about it.\n\nThat part really bothers me. I feel embarrassed, honestly. These are groups I care about, and I don’t want to be unreliable. But lately I feel like people don’t quite trust that they can count on me the way they used to, and that makes me feel awful. So yes, it’s caused a lot of distress, and it’s definitely interfering with my normal life.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced this kind of restlessness or need to move when you started, stopped, or changed the dose of a medication, even if it was a long time ago?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I’ve been trying to think back, because I know you’re asking whether there was some clear point where I started or stopped a medicine, or the dose changed, and then this restlessness showed up.\n\nBut I can’t really say that. I don’t have a memory of, “Oh, that happened right after a medication change.” I just don’t want to guess and accidentally make it sound like there’s a connection when I haven’t actually noticed one.\n\nI mean, it may be that there were medication changes over the years and I just didn’t pay attention closely enough, but as I’m sitting here trying to remember, nothing stands out to me like that. What stands out is the restlessness itself and how hard it’s been to settle down, but I can’t tie it to starting, stopping, or changing a dose of something.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel driven to do certain things over and over, like checking, counting, or fixing, because you can't get certain thoughts out of your head or because you feel something bad will happen if you don't?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in the way you’re describing. I mean, I do sometimes go back and recheck things, but I think that’s more because I get distracted or I don’t trust that I finished something properly, not because I have a specific thought like, “If I don’t check this three times, something terrible will happen.”\n\nI don’t really count things or have rituals like that, at least not that I’ve noticed. And I don’t feel like I’m fixing things over and over to stop some bad event from happening. It’s more that I’ll lose track, or I’ll feel unsettled and then I’m not sure what I already did. But I can’t say I have those stuck thoughts you’re talking about, where they force me to do a certain action in a certain way.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble sitting still or feeling calm, even going back to when you were younger, or is this something that started more recently in your life?\n\nPatient: Oh, it goes way back. It’s not something that just started recently, even though it feels worse or more noticeable now.\n\nI actually remember seeing comments from teachers when I was in grade school — or my mother would mention them — that I was “bright but careless,” that kind of thing. They’d say I lost papers, talked when I wasn’t supposed to, didn’t stay with my work, or needed reminders to stay in my seat and finish what I was doing. At the time, I think people just thought I was careless or not trying hard enough, because I could understand the schoolwork, but I was always being corrected for the way I went about it.\n\nSo yes, the sitting still and settling myself, that has been there since I was young. It’s just that when you’re a child, people scold you or redirect you, and you don’t necessarily think of it as a pattern until much later.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period lasting several days or more where you felt extremely upbeat, unusually confident, or so energetic that you hardly needed sleep?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean, I’ve had times where I felt keyed up or couldn’t settle, but not in the way you’re saying — like several days where I was unusually upbeat or full of confidence and barely sleeping and still feeling great.\n\nI’m trying to be careful, because my energy can feel all over the place, and sometimes I’ll stay up too late or get wound up, but I don’t remember a stretch where I thought, “Wow, I don’t need sleep,” or where I was on top of the world for days. If anything, when I don’t sleep enough, I usually feel worse, not better.\n\nSo as far as I can tell, no, I haven’t had that kind of period.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when your mood was elevated, you felt unusually good or irritable for several days, and you were doing things more quickly, taking more chances, or talking more than usual?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve had a time like that. It’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic, but it’s like I’m in my own skin and it doesn’t feel quiet in there. Even when I’m tired — and I mean really worn out, where I know I should just sit down and rest — there’s this feeling like I have to get up, do the next thing, move on to something else, check something, start something.\n\nIt’s not just being “busy,” you know? It feels more like an inner push, like I’m being prodded from the inside. I’ll tell myself, “Just sit still for a minute,” and then almost right away I feel like I need to jump up again. I wouldn’t say I’ve had, like, a clear stretch where I suddenly felt wonderful and full of energy and didn’t need sleep.
